--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 15630) has a good structure:
- State 0: Repulsion (keeps moving, spreading out)
- State 1: Exploration (keeps moving)
- State 2: Stop (waits still)

The key insight is that state 2 is the "stop" state, and it transitions back to the exploration state via a **green color detection** (C=7, l=3 which is... wait, l=3 is blue? Let me check: colors are {1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}). The green block is color 2. So the champion uses l=3 (blue) for color detection - that's not quite right for detecting the GREEN block.

Wait, the champion's transition `--n2x1 3 --c2x1 7 --p2x1 0.0271 --l2x1 3` goes from state 2 BACK to state 0, meaning it leaves the stop state when it sees blue (not green). The transition INTO stop state is via random probability (`--c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.1649`) and neighbor count.

The critical transition: from state 1 (exploration) to state 2 (stop) should be triggered by seeing GREEN (color 2). Looking at the champion's state 1 transitions: they use floor conditions and probability - NOT color detection for green! This means the champion doesn't properly detect the green block.

I need to build machines where:
1. Moving states (exploration/repulsion) in phase 1
2. Transition to STOP state triggered by detecting GREEN (color 2, C=7, l=2)
3. High probability on the green detection so robots stop quickly

Let me design better FSMs:

**Strategy**: State 0 = Exploration (moving), State 1 = Stop. Transition 0→1 triggered by C=7, l=2 (green detection) with high probability. State 1 has a very small probability of going back to state 0 (or stays put).

For the 14 machines:
1. Champion (exact copy)
2-6. Exploitation of champion (small tweaks)
7-14. Exploration with proper green-detection transitions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is a simple 2-state FSM:
- State 0: GoToColor (green, color 2) - moves toward the green block, keeping robots moving in phase 1
- State 1: Stop - stands still
- Transition 0→1: C=7 (perceive green, color 2) with p=0.95 - when green block appears, transition to stop
- Transition 0→1: C=5 (random) with p=0.002 - tiny fallback
- Transition 1→0: C=5 (random) with p=0.001 - almost never leaves stop

This is elegant: GoToColor(green) keeps robots moving AND reacts to the green signal automatically. The score of 9239 is much better than the old champion.

Key improvements to try:
1. Make the stop state even stickier (p=0.0001 or 0 effectively)
2. Try higher green detection probability (0.98, 0.99)
3. Try different moving behaviors (Exploration, Repulsion, Attraction) with green detection
4. Try adding neighbor-count condition to reinforce stopping
5. Try 3-state variants where a second moving state helps coverage

The main insight: the random fallback probability of 0.002 from stop back to moving causes unnecessary movement in phase 2. Reducing it to near 0 should help. Also, the 0.95 detection probability means ~5% miss rate per step - could increase to 0.98 or 0.99.

Let me also consider: GoToColor(green) means robots move TOWARD the green wall. This is fine for phase 1. When green appears, they detect it and stop. The p=0.95 means on average ~20 steps delay after the signal.

**Exploitation**: Vary p0x0 (0.95→0.98, 0.99, 1.0), p0x1 (0.002→0.001, 0.0005), p1x0 (0.001→0.0001), try clr0=2 with different base behaviors.

**Exploration**: 3-state machines where state 0 is exploration, state 1 is GoToColor(green), state 2 is stop, with transitions driven by green detection.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is a clean 2-state FSM:
- State 0: GoToColor(green) - keeps robots moving toward the green wall
- Transition 0→1: C=7 (perceive green, color 2) with p=0.99 - very high probability of detecting green
- Transition 0→1: C=5 (random) with p=0.001 - tiny fallback
- State 1: Stop - stands still
- Transition 1→0: C=5 (random) with p=0.0005 - almost never leaves stop

The key pattern is clear: GoToColor(green) as the moving state works well because it both keeps robots moving AND detects the green signal. The p=0.99 detection is near-optimal.

**What to try to improve further:**
1. Push p0x0 to 1.0 (maximum detection probability) - eliminates the 1% miss rate
2. Reduce the random fallback p0x1 even lower (0.0001, 0.0005) or remove it
3. Reduce p1x0 to essentially 0 (0.0001) to make stop state stickier
4. Try with no random fallback from state 0 (single transition only on green detection)
5. Try adding a neighbor-count condition to reinforce stopping in state 1
6. Try GoAwayColor(green) - robots flee green wall while it's gray, then stop when they see green
7. Try different LED colors (cosmetic but may affect condition 7 detection)

The worst performers confirm: Exploration-based moving states are much worse than GoToColor(green), because GoToColor naturally orients toward the signal.

**Key insight**: The random probability transition from state 0 (p=0.001) means ~1 robot per 1000 steps might accidentally stop - at 28 robots over ~500 phase-1 steps, that's ~14 accidental stops. Removing it or making it 0.0001 could help.

Also: p=0.99 detection per step means after the green appears, expected delay is 1/0.99 ≈ 1 step per robot. That's nearly perfect. Can we do better? p=1.0 would be instant.

Let me also consider: maybe the issue is robots that can't SEE the green block (it's behind them or occluded). Adding a second transition back to GoToColor from Stop when green is NOT visible could be problematic. Let's keep it simple.

################################################################################
# ROUND 5/10
################################################################################

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion (8234) has an interesting addition: state 1 (Stop) has TWO transitions:
- C=5 (random) p=0.0005 - tiny chance to return to moving
- C=3 (neighbors ABOVE threshold 8, steep sigmoid w=10) - if too many neighbors cluster, go back to moving

This makes sense: if robots cluster (many neighbors), some go back to moving to spread out, reducing accidental movement. But wait - in phase 2 we WANT robots stopped. The neighbor condition might be helping because when stopped robots cluster, some re-enter the moving state which... costs points. Hmm.

Actually, looking more carefully: the neighbor condition C=3 with p=8 (threshold=8 neighbors) and w=10 (steep) would trigger when a robot has >8 neighbors. With 28 robots in a small arena, this might help avoid clustering that causes collisions/forced movement. But in phase 2 we want stopped - this seems counterproductive.

The score improvement (8234 vs 8419) suggests it IS helping. Perhaps the neighbor condition in the stop state helps escape situations where robots are being physically pushed (collisions) and the "stop" is being violated anyway - by briefly moving they can reposition.

**Key observations:**
1. Single transition only (no random fallback) is BAD (scores 11517-11538) - the fallback p=0.001 from state 0 is important
2. p=0.99 detection beats p=1.0 slightly
3. The neighbor-count exit from stop state helps

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary the neighbor threshold (6, 7, 8, 9, 10) and steepness in state 1
- Vary p0x1 (the random fallback from moving: 0.001, 0.002, 0.003)
- Try adding a green-detection exit from stop back to moving (if they lose sight of green, return to GoToColor)
- Try adding neighbor condition to state 0 as well

**Exploration:**
- 3-state machines with an intermediate "slow down" state
- Try C=4 (neighbors BELOW threshold) in stop state - leave stop if isolated

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (7450) has these key features:
- State 0: GoToColor(green) with p=0.99 green detection → Stop, and p=0.002 random fallback
- State 1: Stop with p=0.0001 random return to moving, AND C=3 (neighbors≥8, steep w=10) return to moving

The key improvement from round 4→5 was reducing p1x0 from 0.0005 to 0.0001. The neighbor condition (C=3, threshold=8, w=10) helps robots escape overcrowding.

**What we know:**
- p0x1=0.002 (random fallback from GoToColor) is good; 0.003 is worse
- p1x0=0.0001 (random exit from stop) beats 0.0005
- C=3 with threshold=8, w=10 in stop state is beneficial
- C=4 (neighbors BELOW threshold) in stop state is bad
- Adding green-detection exit from stop (C=7, l=2) is bad
- Adding extra neighbor condition in stop state is bad (3 transitions worse than 2)

**Exploitation:**
- Try p1x0 even lower: 0.00005, 0.00001
- Try different neighbor thresholds: 7, 8, 9, 10 with w=10, 12, 15, 20
- Try p0x1 slightly different: 0.0015, 0.0025
- Try p0x0=0.995 (between 0.99 and 1.0)

**Exploration:**
- Try adding a 3rd transition from state 0 (neighbor-based) to help clustering detection before stopping
- Try 3-state FSM where state 2 is repulsion (spreading) → transitions to stop on green
- Try varying the neighbor threshold combinations

The neighbor condition in stop state (C=3, threshold=8) fires when robot has ≥8 neighbors out of 28 robots in a small arena. Since range is 0.44m in a 0.87m radius arena, 8 neighbors is quite dense. This makes robots briefly move when overcrowded, which may actually reduce collisions that force movement anyway.

## Reasoning

The champion (7293.93) uses threshold=7 for the neighbor condition in stop state, while threshold=8 gives 7294.93 (nearly identical). The scores are very close - we're in a fine-tuning regime.

**Key findings so far:**
- The 2-state GoToColor(green)→Stop structure is optimal
- p0x0=0.99 (green detection from moving state) is best
- p0x1=0.002 (random fallback from moving) is best (0.0015 and 0.001 are worse)
- p1x0=0.0001 (random exit from stop) is best
- C=3 (neighbors≥threshold, steep w=10+) in stop state helps
- Threshold=7 is slightly better than 8 (both near-optimal)
- w=10, 12, 15, 20 all give same score (7294.93) vs threshold=7 giving 7293.93
- 3-state machines are worse

**What to explore:**
1. Fine-tune threshold: try 5, 6, 7 (we know 7 is best so far)
2. Fine-tune steepness w with threshold=7: try w=8, 12, 15, 20
3. Try p0x1 slightly higher: 0.0025 (between 0.002 and 0.003)
4. Try p1x0 even lower: 0.00005, 0.00001
5. Try adding a third transition from state 0: C=3 (many neighbors→stop faster)
6. Try different combinations of two neighbor conditions in stop state
7. Try w=10 with threshold=6 and 5

The scores are converging around 7294-7450. The difference between threshold=7 (7293.93) and threshold=8 (7294.93) is exactly 1 point - this is likely noise or a very small systematic effect.

## Reasoning

The champion (7293.93) has held for two rounds. This round's best performers all scored ~7614, which is worse than the champion. This suggests we're near a local optimum with the current structure.

**Key observations:**
- The champion score of 7293.93 was achieved in round 6 with threshold=7, w=10
- Round 7 re-ran the same parameters and got 7614.65 - this is stochastic variation! The true score is somewhere around 7300-7600 range
- All variants of threshold=7 (w=8,10,12,15,20) give essentially the same score (~7614)
- p0x0=0.995 gives 7614.63, nearly identical to 0.99
- Adding extra transitions to state 0 is worse
- 3-state machines are worse

**What this tells us:** We're very close to the optimum. The stochasticity (~300 point variance) makes it hard to distinguish variants. The champion's score of 7293 may be slightly lucky, but the structure is right.

**Strategy for round 8:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Re-evaluate best variants multiple times (they'll average out better)
3. Try threshold=7 with p1x0=0 effectively (not possible, minimum is some small value)
4. Try completely different structures as exploration - maybe there's something much better we haven't tried
5. Try GoAwayColor from a non-green color while moving, stopping on green
6. Try having the stop state use C=3 with threshold=6 (slightly lower density trigger)
7. Try p0x1=0.002 vs removing the random fallback entirely (we know single transition is bad, but maybe with the neighbor condition it's different)

Actually, let me think about what's limiting performance:
- Phase 1: robots need to move. GoToColor(green) moves them.
- Phase 2: robots need to stop. They detect green and stop.
- The ~7300 score means roughly 7300/(28*1200) ≈ 0.217 points per robot per step wasted.

The main cost is likely: (1) robots accidentally stopping in phase 1 (from p0x1=0.002 fallback), (2) robots not detecting green immediately in phase 2, (3) robots leaving stop state (from p1x0=0.0001 or neighbor condition).

Let me try: reduce p0x1 below 0.002, try 0.001 again but with the neighbor condition (previously 0.001 was worse WITHOUT the neighbor condition in stop - now try WITH it).

Also try: What if the neighbor condition threshold is lower (5, 6) - triggers more often but helps escape crowding.

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The scores are converging tightly around 7293-7313. Multiple variants of the champion topology (threshold 6,7,8,9,10 with w=10) all score within ~20 points of each other. This is essentially noise - the stochastic simulation variance is larger than the parameter differences.

**Key findings:**
- Threshold 7-10 all give ~7293-7302 (within noise)
- Threshold 6 gives 7304, threshold 5 slightly worse
- The structure is locked in: GoToColor(green) → Stop, with neighbor-exit from stop
- w=10 is consistently good; higher w doesn't hurt much
- p0x1=0.002 is optimal; 0.001 and 0.0025 are worse
- p1x0=0.0001 is optimal

**What might give a genuine improvement:**
1. The random fallback p0x1=0.002 from moving state costs ~28 robots × 500 steps × 0.002 = 28 accidental stops in phase 1. Can we eliminate this? Previously, single-transition machines scored ~11500 - but those didn't have the neighbor condition. Wait, those were different - they had no random fallback AND no neighbor condition. What if we have NO random fallback but DO have the neighbor condition in the stop state? But the issue is the random fallback is from state 0 (moving), not state 1 (stop).

Actually the p0x1=0.002 random probability from state 0 (GoToColor) to state 1 (Stop) means robots accidentally stop during phase 1. This is bad. But without it, robots only stop when they see green - and if they're facing away from the green wall at the moment it appears, they might never stop (GoToColor will eventually turn them toward it).

2. What about trying a different clr for state 0? Using GoToColor with a different color (like yellow, which isn't in the arena) would make robots move randomly around the arena, then stop on green.

3. Try GoAwayColor(green) - robots move away from green while arena is all-gray (no green to see), then stop when green appears.

Wait - GoAwayColor when there's no green would the robot just wander? Actually if there's no green block visible, GoAwayColor might behave like random exploration. Then when green appears, robots detect it and stop. But the transition would need to be from GoAwayColor state to Stop on C=7, l=2.

4. Try using a non-existent color for GoToColor to get exploration-like behavior while still having green detection.

5. Try with threshold 9 or 10 (both scored 7300.42, best this round) - re-evaluate multiple times.

Let me focus on:
- 4 copies of champion (elitism + statistical averaging)
- Variants with threshold 9, 10 (best this round)
- Try p0x1=0.002 with threshold 9/10 at different w values
- Exploration: try GoAwayColor(green) as moving state
- Try: no random fallback, just green detection from moving state with VERY high p (1.0)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. The champion (7293.93) has held since round 6. The structure is completely locked in - 2-state GoToColor(green)→Stop with neighbor-exit from stop.

**Final analysis:**
- The champion uses threshold=7, w=10. This round's best was threshold=9 with GoAwayColor (7394) - but still worse than champion.
- The GoAwayColor(green) variant (7394) is interesting - when no green exists, GoAwayColor might behave like exploration, then stops when green appears. But 7394 > 7293.
- All threshold variants (7,8,9,10) score within ~7293-7510 range (stochastic noise)
- 3-state machines are consistently worse
- Replacing random fallback (p0x1) with neighbor condition in state 0 is BAD (11519!)

**For the final round, I should:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Run champion topology multiple times for statistical averaging
3. Try small variations around the winning parameters
4. Try a couple of genuine explorations in case there's a much better solution

**Parameters to fine-tune:**
- Threshold: try 7, 8, 9, 10 (all competitive)
- p1x0: try 0.00005, 0.0001
- p0x1: 0.002 seems optimal
- p0x0: 0.99 seems optimal
- w1x1: try 10, 12, 15 with threshold 7

**One unexplored idea**: What if the stop state has the neighbor condition AND also a color-detection transition back to GoToColor? If robots in stop lose sight of green (turn around), they briefly re-enter GoToColor to face green again, then immediately stop. But color detection exit from stop was shown to be bad earlier.

**Another idea**: Try clr0=2 (GoToColor green) vs clr0=1 (GoToColor red) - maybe heading toward a non-existent color keeps robots moving more randomly and they cover the arena better before phase 2.

Let me run the champion topology with threshold 7 multiple times, and try a few variations:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.87 m. 28 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.59 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.44 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
